\mathbb{P}(Z_{0}>t)\leq\frac{1}{t}{\rm{exp}}(-t^{2}/2)
. Therefore, we have
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\psi_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}^{s}=1)\to 0
.

Under
H_{1}
,
T_{sum}\sim N(\mu_{sum},1)
with
\mu_{sum}:=\frac{\lambda\breve{\mathbf{k}}}{\sqrt{\breve{\mathbf{n}}}}
.

Then if (
14
) holds,

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(T_{sum}\leq W)&=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(Z_{\mu_{sum}-W}\leq 0)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(Z_{(1-c)\mu_{sum}}\leq 0)\to 0,\end{split}

(75)

where the last inequality is by assumption
W=c\mu_{sum}(c<1)
when (
14
) holds.

If (
15
) holds, then

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(T_{scan}\leq T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}})&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}({\mathbfcal{Y}}^{sum}_{C^{*}}\leq T_{\mathbf{k}})\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(Z_{\lambda\sqrt{\breve{\mathbf{k}}}-T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}}}\leq 0)=\Phi(T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}}-\lambda\sqrt{\breve{\mathbf{k}}}),\end{split}

(76)

where
C^{*}=\mathcal{S}({\mathbfcal{X}})
denote the true latent support of
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
given
{\mathbfcal{Y}}\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{X}}}
.

Under assumption (
A1
), by Stirling formula, we have

T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}}=\sqrt{2\log(G^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{k}})}\sim\sqrt{2\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\log\frac{n_{i}}{k_{i}}}.

So
T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}}-\lambda\sqrt{\breve{\mathbf{k}}}\to-\infty
and
\Phi(T_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}}-\lambda\sqrt{\breve{\mathbf{k}}})\to 0
under condition (
15
).

Combining (
75
) and (
76
), we have
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(\psi_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}^{s}=0)\to 0
holds for every
{\mathbfcal{X}}
, so

\sup_{{\mathbfcal{X}}\in\mathscr{X}_{{\rm{CHC}}}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(\psi_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}^{s}=0)\to 0.

This has finished the proof.

B.3
Proof of Theorem
13

Similar to the proof of Theorem
11
, we only need to consider two tests
\psi_{max}
and
\psi_{sum}
separately.

As we have shown in Theorem
11
, when
W\to\infty
,
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\psi_{sum}=1)\to 0
. Also
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(\psi_{sum}=0)\to 0,\,\forall{\mathbfcal{X}}\in\mathscr{X}_{{\rm{CHC}}}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)
when condition (
14
) holds. So we only need to show
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\psi_{max}=1)\to 0
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(\psi_{max}=0)\to 0\text{ for }{\mathbfcal{X}}\in\mathscr{X}_{{\rm{CHC}}}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)
.

First

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{0}(\psi_{max}=1)&=\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(T_{max}\geq\sqrt{2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log n_{i}}\right)\\
&=\breve{\mathbf{n}}\mathbb{P}(Z_{0}\geq\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}})\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}}}\to 0,\end{split}

(77)

where the last inequality is due to
\mathbb{P}(Z_{0}>t)\leq\frac{1}{t}{\rm{exp}}(-t^{2}/2)
. Also

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(\psi_{max}=0)&=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(T_{max}<\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}})\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(Z_{\lambda}\leq\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}})\\
&=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbfcal{X}}(Z_{0}\leq\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}}-\lambda)\to 0,\end{split}

(78)

where the last term goes to
0
because
\sqrt{2\log\breve{\mathbf{n}}}\to\infty
and condition (
17
). This has finished the proof.

B.4
Proof of Theorem
10

To prove the lower bound, it is enough to prove for the following case

H_{0}:{\mathbfcal{X}}=0\quad\text{ vs }\quad H_{1}:{\mathbfcal{X}}=\mu\cdot\mathbf{v}\circ\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v},

where
\mathbf{v}
is in the set
\mathcal{C}
of
k
-sparse unit vector with non-zero entries equal to
\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}
. In this case,
{\mathbfcal{Y}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
under
H_{0}
and
{\mathbfcal{Y}}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{v}}:=\mathcal{L}(\mu\cdot\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})})
under
H_{1}
.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{1}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{v}}
, so the likelihood ratio is

\begin{split}\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{1}}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}&={\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{n}\cdots\sum_{i_{d}=1}^{n}\left({\mathbfcal{Y}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}-\mu\mathbf{v}_{i_{1}}\ldots\mathbf{v}_{i_{d}}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{n}\cdots\sum_{i_{d}=1}^{n}{\mathbfcal{Y}}^{2}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}\right)\\
&={\rm{exp}}\left(\mu\langle{\mathbfcal{Y}},\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\rangle-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\|\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\|_{{\rm{HS}}}^{2}\right)\\
&={\rm{exp}}\left(\mu\langle{\mathbfcal{Y}},\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\rangle-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\right),\end{split}

where the last inequality is due to the fact that
\|\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\|_{{\rm{HS}}}=1
.

As we illustrate in (
53
), the result follows by showing
\mathbb{E}_{0}\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{1}}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}\right)^{2}\to 1
. Notice

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{0}\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{1}}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}\right)^{2}&=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{u}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{v}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{0}{\rm{exp}}\left(\mu\langle{\mathbfcal{Y}},\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{u}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}\rangle-\mu^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}{\rm{exp}}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\|\mathbf{u}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\|_{{\rm{HS}}}^{2}-\mu^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}{\rm{exp}}\left(\mu^{2}\langle\mathbf{u}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}\rangle\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{C}}{\rm{exp}}\left(\mu^{2}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v})^{d}\right)\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\sim\text{Uinf}[\mathcal{C}]}\left({\rm{exp}}(\mu^{2}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v})^{d})\right),\end{split}

here the third equality follows from
\mathbb{E}({\rm{exp}}(\langle t,{\mathbfcal{Y}}))={\rm{exp}}(\frac{1}{2}\|t\|_{{\rm{HS}}}^{2})
. Let
G_{m}
denote a symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
stopped at the
m^{th}
step. When
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\sim\text{Unif}[\mathcal{C}]
are independent,
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
follows distribution of
\frac{G_{H}}{k}
where
H
follows Hypergeometric distribution with parameter
(n,k,k)
. Thus
\mu^{2}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v})^{d}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{k^{d}}G_{H}^{d}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{k\log\frac{en}{k}}\frac{\log\frac{en}{k}}{k^{d-1}}G_{H}^{d}
. Then when
\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{k\log\frac{en}{k}}}\to 0
, by Lemma
8
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\sim\text{Uinf}[\mathcal{C}]}\left({\rm{exp}}(\mu^{2}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v})^{d})\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm{exp}}(\frac{\mu^{2}}{k\log\frac{en}{k}}\frac{\log\frac{en}{k}}{k^{d-1}}G_{H}^{d})\right]\to 1.

So this has finished the proof of this theorem.

Lemma 8

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in[n]
. Let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
be independently Rademacher distributed. Denote the symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
stopped at the
m^{th}
step by

G_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}B_{i}.

Let
H\sim\text{Hypergeometric}(n,k,k)
with
\mathbb{P}(H=i)=\frac{{k\choose i}{n-k\choose k-i}}{{n\choose k}},i=1,\ldots,k
. Then there exists a function
g:(0,c)\to(1,\infty)
with
c>0
is a fixed small constant and
g(0+)=1
, such that for any
a<c
,

\mathbb{E}{\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})\leq g(a),

(79)

where
t=\frac{a}{k^{d-1}}\log\frac{en}{k}
.

B.4.1
Proof of Lemma
8

When
d=2
, this Lemma was proved in
Cai, Ma and Wu 2015
. Their proof heavily relies on the explicit formula of a moment generating function (MGF) of the second moment of Gaussian random variables, which can not be extended to the
d\geq 3
case as the MGF of high-order moment of Gaussian random variables does not exist. Here, we introduce a new way of proving this lemma. Throughout the proof, we assume
a
is sufficiently small and
k,n
are sufficiently large.

Recall that for non-negative random variable
Y
,
\mathbb{E}(Y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(Y\geq x)dx
. Condition on
H
,
0<{\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})\leq{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})
, thus

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})|H\right]&=\int_{0}^{{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})}\mathbb{P}\left({\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})\geq x\right)dx\\
&=\int_{0}^{{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})}\mathbb{P}\left(G_{H}\geq\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\right)dx\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})}\mathbb{P}\left(|G_{H}|\geq\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\right)dx\\
&=\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})}\mathbb{P}\left(|G_{H}|\geq\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\right)dx+1\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(tH^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2H}\right)dx+1.\end{split}

(80)

Here (a) is due to Hoeffding’s inequality.

By taking expectation over
H
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})\right]\leq\sum_{0\leq h\leq k}\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm{exp}}(tG_{H}^{d})|H=h\right]\mathbb{P}(H=h)+1.

(81)

To prove the result, we only need to show the right hand side of (
81
) is upper bounded by
g(a)
, or equivalently we can show that there exists
f(a)>0
with
f(0+)=0
such that

\sum_{0\leq h\leq k}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(th^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2h}\right)dx\mathbb{P}(H=h)\leq f(a).

(82)

The idea to prove this is to divide the summation in (
82
) into three parts and bound each of them.

Small h
. Assume
h\leq C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}
. In this regime,

\begin{split}&\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:0\leq h\leq C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(th^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2h}\right)dx\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\leq\max_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:0\leq h\leq C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(th^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2h}\right)dx\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}2\left({\rm{exp}}\left(Ct\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)-1\right)\\
&\overset{(b)}{\leq}{\rm{exp}}(\frac{Ca}{e})-1,\end{split}

(83)

(a) is because the integrand is less or equal to 1 and the fourth inequality is due to the fact
\left(Ct\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)=\frac{Ca\log\frac{en}{k}}{k^{d-1}}\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}=\frac{Ca}{\log\frac{en}{k}}\leq\frac{Ca}{e}
.

Moderate large h
. Assume
C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}
. In this regime,

\begin{split}&\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(th^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2h}\right)dx\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}}\left(C\left({\rm{exp}}(th^{d}-h)+{\rm{exp}}(-K)\right)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})\right)\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}}\left(C\left(({\rm{exp}}(-ch)+{\rm{exp}}(-K)\right)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})\right)\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(c)}{\leq}C{\rm{exp}}(-K)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})+\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:C\left(\frac{k^{d-1}}{\log^{2}\frac{en}{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}}C{\rm{exp}}(-ch)\\
&\leq{\rm{exp}}(a)-1,\end{split}

(84)

here the (a) is due to Lemma
9
and
K=\frac{a^{\frac{2}{d}}k^{\frac{2d-2}{d}}}{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}h}
, (b) is due to the fact that when
h\leq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}
, we have
th^{d}\leq h
, (c) is due to the fact that
\mathbb{P}(H=h)\leq 1
and
\sum_{h}\mathbb{P}(H=h)\leq 1
and the last inequality holds for sufficiently large
k
.

Large h
. Assume
\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k
,

\begin{split}&\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(th^{d})}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{\left(\frac{\log x}{t}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}{2h}\right)dx\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}\left(C\left({\rm{exp}}(th^{d}-h)+{\rm{exp}}(-K)\right)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})\right)\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}\left(C\left({\rm{exp}}(cth^{d})+{\rm{exp}}(-K)\right)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})\right)\mathbb{P}(H=h)\\
&\overset{(c)}{\leq}C\left({\rm{exp}}(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}})+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{H\sim H\mathcal{G}}\left[C{\rm{exp}}(ctH^{d})\mathbf{1}(H\geq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}})\right]\\
&\overset{(d)}{\leq}C\left({\rm{exp}}(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}})+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{H\sim{\rm{Bin}}}\left[C{\rm{exp}}(ctH^{d})\mathbf{1}(H\geq\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}})\right]\\
&=C\left({\rm{exp}}(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}})+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right)\\
&\quad+\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}C{\rm{exp}}(cth^{d})\left(\frac{k}{n-k}\right)^{h}\left(\frac{n-2k}{n-k}\right)^{k-h}{k\choose h}\\
&\overset{(e)}{\leq}C\left({\rm{exp}}(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}})+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right)+\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}{\rm{exp}}(ctk^{d-1}h-h\log\frac{n}{2k}+h\log\frac{ek}{h})\\
&\overset{(f)}{\leq}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{k}{a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}}\leq h\leq k}{\rm{exp}}\left(cah\log\frac{en}{k}-h\log\frac{n}{2k}+h\log\left(ea^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\right)\right)\\
&\quad+C\left({\rm{exp}}(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}})+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right)\\
&\overset{(g)}{\leq}{\rm{exp}}(a)-1,\end{split}

(85)

here (a) is due to Lemma
9
with
K=\frac{a^{\frac{2}{d}}k^{\frac{2d-2}{d}}}{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}h}
, (b) is due to the fact that when
h\geq a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
,
th^{d}\geq h
, (c) is due to the fact that

C{\rm{exp}}(-K)+\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})\leq C\left({\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}{(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}\right)+\frac{2(\log\frac{en}{k})^{\frac{2}{d}}}{k^{\frac{d-2}{d}}}\right),

(d) is due to stochastic dominance of Hypergeometric distribution Hypergeometric
(n,k,k)
by Binomial distribution
{\rm{Bin}}(k,\frac{k}{n-k})
(e.g., see Lemma 5.2 of
Butucea and
Ingster 2013
), (e) is due to that
{k\choose h}\leq\left(\frac{ek}{h}\right)^{h}
and
n\geq 3k
and
\frac{n-2k}{n-k}<1
, (f) is due to that in the summation range of
h
,
\frac{k}{h}\leq a^{\frac{1}{d-1}}\left(\log\frac{en}{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
and (g) holds for a sufficient small
a
and sufficiently large
k
.

So we can take
f(a)=2({\rm{exp}}(a)-1)+{\rm{exp}}(\frac{Ca}{e})-1
, i.e.,
g(a)=2({\rm{exp}}(a)-1)+{\rm{exp}}(\frac{Ca}{e})
in the statement and this has finished the proof.

Lemma 9

Suppose
M
,
K
are sufficiently large fixed constants and set
\tau=\frac{2}{d}
, then for
d\geq 3
, we have

\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(M)}2{\rm{exp}}(-K\log^{\tau}x)dx\leq\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K})+C\left({\rm{exp}}(-K)+{\rm{exp}}(-M^{\tau}K+M)\right),

for some
C>0
.

B.4.2
Proof of Lemma
9

The idea to prove this lemma is to divide the integral range into different parts and then bound each part separately.

\begin{split}\int_{1}^{{\rm{exp}}(M)}2{\rm{exp}}(-K\log^{\tau}x)dx&=\int_{1}^{e}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-K\log^{\tau}x\right)dx+\sum_{i=1}^{M^{\tau}-1}\int_{{\rm{exp}}(i^{\frac{1}{\tau}})}^{{\rm{exp}}((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}})}2{\rm{exp}}(-K\log^{\tau}x)dx\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}\int_{1}^{e}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-K\log^{\tau}x\right)dx+2\sum_{i=1}^{M^{\tau}-1}{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right),\end{split}

(86)

where (a) is due to the fact that the integrand decays as
x
increases.

We first bound the first term in the right hand side of (
86
). Since
\log x\in[0,1]
when
x\in[1,e]
and
\tau<1
, we have

\begin{split}&\int_{1}^{e}2{\rm{exp}}\left(-K\log^{\tau}x\right)dx\leq\int_{1}^{e}2{\rm{exp}}(-K\log x)dx\\
=&\int_{1}^{e}2x^{-K}dx=\frac{2}{K-1}(1-e^{1-K}).\end{split}

Now we move onto the second term in (
86
). We show that the value of the sequence of
{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)
is a U-shape curve, specifically, it first geometrically decreases and then geometrically increases, so the summation of the sequence could be upper bounded by the sum of first term and the last term.

Compare two consecutive values in the summation
\sum_{i=1}^{M^{\tau}-1}{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)
, when
i
is large,

\begin{split}\frac{{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)}{{\rm{exp}}(-(i-1)K){\rm{exp}}\left(i^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)}&={\rm{exp}}(-K){\rm{exp}}\left(i^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\left[(1+\frac{1}{i})^{\frac{1}{\tau}}-1\right]\right)\\
&={\rm{exp}}(-K){\rm{exp}}\left(C\frac{1}{\tau}i^{\frac{1}{\tau}-1}\right),\end{split}

for some
C>0
, where the last equation is due to Taylor expansion.

So the sequence
{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)
geometrically decreases when
i\lesssim K^{\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}}
and then geometrically increases when
i\gtrsim K^{\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}}
. At the same time, the beginning terms in the series
{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)
are of order
{\rm{exp}}(-K)
.

So by the summation property of geometric sequence, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{M^{\tau}-1}{\rm{exp}}(-iK){\rm{exp}}\left((i+1)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)\leq C\left({\rm{exp}}(-K)+{\rm{exp}}(-M^{\tau}K+M)\right),

for some
C>0
. Combining two parts, we finish the proof of this Lemma.

B.5
Proof of Theorem
12

Notice that by Theorem
5
, when
\mu\geq C\sqrt{k\log n}
, the
{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}
search Algorithm
2
can identify the true support with probability goes to 1. Then by Lemma
10
,
\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\psi_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}^{s})\to 0
follows by observing that
\psi_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}^{s}
is the algorithm used in Lemma
10
. Combining them together, we get the theorem.

B.6
Proof of Theorem
14

First in regime
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2(\prod_{i=1}^{d}k_{i})(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log n_{i})}}>1
, by the same argument as Theorem
13
, we can show
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\psi_{max}=1)\to 1
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{X}}}(\psi_{max}=0)\to 0
.

In regime
\mu\geq Cn^{\frac{d}{4}}
, notice that by Theorem
7
, the Power-iteration algorithm can identify the true support with probability goes to 1. Also notice that
\mu\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}
in this case, then by Lemma
10
,
\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\psi_{sing})\to 0
follows by observing that
\psi_{sing}
is the algorithm used in Lemma
10
. Combining them together, we get the theorem.

Lemma 10

Consider
{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\mu)
and
{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\mu)
under the asymptotic regime (
A1
).

(1) If
\mu\geq C\sqrt{k\log n}
for some
C>0
and there is a sequence of recovery algorithm
\{\phi_{R}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}}(\phi_{R})<\eta
for
\eta\in(0,1]
, then there exists a sequence of test
\{\phi_{D}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<\eta
.

(2) If
\mu\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}
for some
C>0
and there is a sequence of polynomial-time recovery algorithm
\{\phi_{R}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}}(\phi_{R})<\eta
for
\eta\in(0,1]
, then there exists a sequence of polynomial-time algorithm
\{\phi_{D}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<\eta
.

B.6.1
Proof of Lemma
10

The idea to prove this lemma is to form two independent copies of the observation from the original observation, then use the first copy and the recovery algorithm
\phi_{R}
to find the true support with high probability, finally use the estimated support and the second copy to do test.

First by the property of Gaussian, it is easy to check
{\mathbfcal{A}}:=\frac{{\mathbfcal{Y}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
and
{\mathbfcal{B}}:=\frac{{\mathbfcal{Y}}-{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
are two independent copies with distribution
\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}\right)
if
{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}
.

Proof of Statement (1)
. Based on
{\mathbfcal{A}}
and assumption about algorithm
\phi_{R}
, we have event
E=\{\phi_{R}({\mathbfcal{Y}})=S(\mathbf{v}_{1})\times\cdots\times S(\mathbf{v}_{d})\}
happens with probability more than
1-\eta
. Condition on
E
, denote
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
to the part of
{\mathbfcal{B}}
that restricted to the support
S(\mathbf{v}_{1})\times\cdots\times S(\mathbf{v}_{d})
.

Apply
{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}
Search Algorithm
2
to
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
and in the output of Step 4, we get
(\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{d})
and let
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\frac{\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{1}}{\sqrt{k_{1}}},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}=\frac{\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{d}}{\sqrt{k_{d}}}
. The test procedure is
\phi_{D}=\mathbf{1}\left(\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}\geq C\sqrt{k}\right)
.

Under
H_{0}
,
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
has i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries, by Lemma 5 of
Zhang and Xia 2018
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}^{\top}|\geq C\sqrt{k})\leq{\rm{exp}}(-ck),

for some
C,c>0
.

Under
H_{1}
and regime
\mu\geq C\sqrt{k\log n}
, by Theorem
5
, we have the elementwise sign of
\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
matches
\mathbf{v}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}\geq c
for some
c>0
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1})
. So conditioned on
E
, with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
,

\begin{split}\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}&=\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{d}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v})\geq\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2}}c^{d}\geq C\sqrt{k},\end{split}

(87)

So overall we have

\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})\leq C{\rm{exp}}(-ck)+Cn^{-1}\to 0,

(88)

condition on
E
. On
E^{c}
we have
\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<1
. So the results follow since
E
happens with probability more than
1-\eta
.

Proof of Statement (2)
. Similarly based on
{\mathbfcal{A}}
and assumption about polynomial-time algorithm
\phi_{R}
, we have event
E=\{\phi_{R}({\mathbfcal{Y}})=S(\mathbf{v}_{1})\times\cdots\times S(\mathbf{v}_{d})\}
happens with probability more than
1-\eta
. Define
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
in the same way as before.

Apply Power-iteration (Algorithm
4
) on
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
and get the rank-one approximation
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}
at the Step 5. The test procedure is
\phi_{D}=\mathbf{1}\left(\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}\geq C\sqrt{k}\right)
.

Under
H_{0}
, similarly we have

\mathbb{P}(|\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}^{\top}|\geq C\sqrt{k})\leq{\rm{exp}}(-ck),

for some
C,c>0
.

Under
H_{1}
and regime
\mu\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}
, by Theorem 1 of
Zhang and Xia 2018
, we have
\|\sin\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i})\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\mu}\leq\frac{1}{2}\,(1\leq i\leq d)
w.p. at least
1-C{\rm{exp}}(-ck)
. Here
\|\sin\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i})\|=\sqrt{1-(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i})^{2}}
is used measure the angle between
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i}
. So
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}\geq\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. Conditioned on
E
,

\begin{split}\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\times_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\top}\times\cdots\times_{d}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{d}&=\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{d}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v})\geq\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2})^{d}\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}\geq C\sqrt{k},\end{split}

(89)

with probability at least
1-C{\rm{exp}}(-ck)
for some
c,C>0
. So overall we have

\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})\leq C{\rm{exp}}(-ck)\to 0,

(90)

condition on
E
. On
E^{c}
,
\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<1
for sufficient large
n
. So the results follow since event
E
happens with probability more than
1-\eta
and it is polynomial-time computable since both
\phi_{R}
and Power-iteration are polynomial-time computable.

C
Average-Case Reduction

We first list some existing tools in the literature and introduce some new results for establishing computational lower bounds.

The following lemma gives the rigorous results why mapping the conjecturally hard problem in distribution to the target problem is the key step in the idea of average-case reduction we have built in Section
5.4
.

Lemma 11
(Lemma 4 of
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2018
)

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be detection problems with hypotheses
H_{0},H_{1},H_{0}^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}
and let
X
and
Y
be instances of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, respectively. Suppose there is a polynomial-time computable map
\varphi
satisfying

{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{H_{0}}(\varphi(X)),\mathcal{L}_{H_{0}^{\prime}}(Y)\right)+\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in H_{1}}\inf_{\pi\in\triangle(H_{1}^{\prime})}{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}}(\varphi(X)),\int_{H_{1}^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(Y)d\pi(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\right)\leq\delta,

where
\triangle(H_{1}^{\prime})
denotes the set of priors on
H_{1}^{\prime}
. If there is a polynomial-time algorithm solving
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with Type I + II error at most
\epsilon
, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm solving
\mathcal{P}
with Type I+II error at most
\epsilon+\delta
.

Lemma 12
(Data Processing
(
Csiszár 1967
)
)

Let
P
and
Q
be distributions on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{B})
and let
f:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
be a Markov transition kernel (see definition in
(
Klenke 2013
, Section 8.3)
). If
A\sim P
and
B\sim Q
, then

{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(f(A)),\mathcal{L}(f(B))\right)\leq{\rm{TV}}(P,Q).

Here,
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
is the distribution of any random variable
``\cdot"
.

Lemma 13
(Tensorization (
Ma and Wu 2015
, Lemma 7))

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
and
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}
be distributions on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{B})
. Then

{\rm{TV}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}P_{i},\prod_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\rm{TV}}(P_{i},Q_{i}).

The following Lemma
14
will be used in proving the computational lower bound of constant high-order cluster recovery.
Given an order-
d
tensor
{\mathbfcal{W}}
, let
{\mathbfcal{W}}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d}}
be the tensor formed by permuting mode
i
indices by permutation
\sigma_{i}
, i.e.

{\mathbfcal{W}}_{[\sigma_{1}(i_{1}),\ldots,\sigma_{d}(i_{d})]}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d}}={\mathbfcal{W}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}.

Lemma 14

Let
P
and
Q
be two distributions such that
Q
dominates
P
, (i.e. for any event
A
,
Q(A)=0
implies
P(A)=0
) and
\chi^{2}(P,Q)\leq 1
. Suppose
{\mathbfcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
is an order-
d
dimension-
n
tensor with all its non-diagonal entries i.i.d. sampled from
Q
and all of its diagonal entries i.i.d. sampled from
P
, where the set of diagonal entries of
{\mathbfcal{W}}
is
\{{\mathbfcal{W}}_{i,i,\ldots,i}\}
. Suppose that
\sigma_{1},\ldots\,\sigma_{d-1}
are independent permutations on
[n]
chosen uniformly at random. Then

{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}),Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\leq\sqrt{\chi^{2}(P,Q)},

where

\chi^{2}(P,Q)=\int\frac{(P(x)-Q(x))^{2}}{Q(x)}dx

is the
\chi^{2}
divergence between distributions
P
and
Q
, and “
{\rm{id}}
" be the identity permutation.

The proof of this lemma is given in Section
C.1
. Next, we introduce the rejection kernel algorithm.

Algorithm 9

Rejection Kernel

1:

Input:

x\in\{0,1\}
, a pair of PMFs or PDFs
f_{X}
and
g_{X}
that can be efficiently computed and sampled, Bernoulli probabilities
p,q\in[0,1]
, number of iterations
T
.

2:

Initialize
Y=0
.

3:

For
i=1,\ldots,T
, do:

(a)

If
x=0
, sample
Z\sim g_{X}
and if

p\cdot g_{X}(Z)\geq q\cdot f_{X}(Z),

then with probability
1-\frac{q\cdot f_{X}(Z)}{p\cdot g_{X}(Z)}
, update
Y=Z
and break.

(b)

If
x=1
, sample
Z\sim f_{X}
and if

(1-q)\cdot f_{X}(Z)\geq(1-p)\cdot g_{X}(Z)

then with probability
1-\frac{(1-p)\cdot g_{X}(Z)}{(1-q)\cdot f_{X}(Z)}
, update
Y=Z
and break.

4:

Output
:
Y
.

Recall we denote the above Rejection Kernel map as
{\rm{RK}}(p\to f_{X},q\to g_{X},T)
. The following lemma discusses the mapping from Bernoulli random variable to Gaussian random variable by rejection kernel. We omit the proof of Lemma
15
here since the proof is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma 14 in
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2018
except for some constant modifications.

Lemma 15

Let
n
be a parameter and suppose that
p=p(n)
and
q=q(n)
satisfy
p>q
,
p,q\in[0,1]
,
\max(q,1-q)=\Omega(1)
and
p-q\geq n^{-O(1)}
. Let
\delta=\min\{\log\frac{p}{q},\log\frac{1-q}{1-p}\}
. Suppose
\xi=\xi(n)\in(0,1)
satisfies

\xi\leq\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log\left((p-q)^{-1}\right)}}.

Then the map
{\rm{RK}}_{G}:={\rm{RK}}(p\to N(\xi,1),q\to N(0,1),T)
with
T=\lceil 2(d+1)\delta^{-1}\log n\rceil

can be computed in poly
(n)
time and satisfies

{\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(p)),N(\xi,1)\right)=O(n^{-(d+1)})\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm{TV}}({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(q),N(0,1)))=O(n^{-(d+1)}).

The next lemma gives the property of tensor reflection cloning in Algorithm
7
.

Lemma 16
(Tensor Reflecting Cloning)

Suppose
n
is even and
\ell=O(\log n)
. There is a randomized polynomial-time computable map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
given by Algorithm
7
that satisfies

1.

\varphi\left(N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
.

2.

Consider any
\lambda>0
and any set of vectors
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. There exists a distribution
\pi
over vectors
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{(\ell)},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}=2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
,
2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}\geq\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq d
such that

\varphi\left(\lambda\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\sim\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}^{d\ell}}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}^{(\ell)}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}^{(\ell)}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)d\pi(\mathbf{u}_{1}^{(\ell)},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}^{(\ell)}),

where
{\mathbfcal{Z}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
. Furthermore, it holds with probability at least
1-4(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|^{-1}_{0})
over
\pi
such that for
i\in[d]
,

2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}\geq\|\mathbf{u}^{(\ell)}_{i}\|_{0}\geq 2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}\left(1-\max(\frac{2C\ell\log(2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0})}{\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}},\frac{2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}}{n})\right),

for some
C>0
if
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}
are sufficiently large and at most
2^{-\ell-1}n
for
i\in[d]
. Finally, if
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{u}_{j}
are equal for some
i,j\in[d],i\neq j
then
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{u}_{j}^{(\ell)}
holds almost surely.

C.1
Proof of Lemma
14

Let
\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}^{\prime}
be independent permutations of
[n]
chosen uniformly at random and also independent of
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}
. For convenience, we denote
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=(\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)),\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)=(\sigma^{\prime}_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}(i))
and
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1})
. By property
\chi^{2}(P,Q)+1=\int\frac{P^{2}(x)}{Q(x)}dx
, we have

\begin{split}&\chi^{2}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}),Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)+1\\
&=\int\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})\right]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{P}_{Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}}({\mathbfcal{X}})}d{\mathbfcal{X}}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}}\int\frac{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1})}{\mathbb{P}_{Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}}({\mathbfcal{X}})}d{\mathbfcal{X}}.\end{split}

(91)

Notice

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{P({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})\prod_{\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)}Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1}]})\right\},

so

\begin{split}&\frac{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1})}{\mathbb{P}_{Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}}({\mathbfcal{X}})}\\
=&\prod_{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)}\left\{\frac{P^{2}({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}{Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}\prod_{\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)}Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1}]})\right\}\\
&\times\prod_{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)}\frac{P({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})P({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma^{\prime}_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}(i)]})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)\\
\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)\end{subarray}}Q^{2}({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1}]})}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)\\
\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)\end{subarray}}Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1}]})}\\
=&\prod_{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)}\frac{P^{2}({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}{Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}\prod_{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)}P({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})P({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma^{\prime}_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}(i)]})\\
&\times\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)\\
\mathbf{j}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)\end{subarray}}Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{d-1}]})\right).\end{split}

(92)

After integration, last two terms at the right hand side of (
92
) are integrated to
1
, only the first term left. So

\begin{split}&\int\frac{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1}}}({\mathbfcal{X}}|\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{d-1})}{\mathbb{P}_{Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}}({\mathbfcal{X}})}d{\mathbfcal{X}}\\
=&\prod_{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)}\left(\int\frac{P^{2}({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}{Q({\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]})}d{\mathbfcal{X}}_{[i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i)]}\right)\\
=&\left(1+\chi^{2}(P,Q)\right)^{|\{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)\}|}.\end{split}

(93)

Let
Y=|\{i:\boldsymbol{\sigma}(i)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)\}|
be the number of fixed coordinates in all
d-1
permutations and let
\bar{Y}=|\{i:\sigma_{1}(i)=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}(i)\}|
be the number of fixed coordinate in the first permutation, clearly
Y\leq\bar{Y}
. As shown in
Pitman 1997
, the
i
th moment of
\bar{Y}
is at most the
i
th Bell
number and possion distribution with rate
1
has its
i
th moment given by
i
th Bell number for all
i
. So the moment generating function(m.g.f.)
\mathbb{E}(e^{t\bar{Y}})
is at most the m.g.f. of possion distribution with rate
1
which is
{\rm{exp}}(e^{t}-1)
. Set
t=\log(1+\chi^{2}(P,Q))
,

\begin{split}\chi^{2}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}),Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)&\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\left(1+\chi^{2}(P,Q)\right)^{Y}]-1\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}[\left(1+\chi^{2}(P,Q)\right)^{\bar{Y}}]-1\\
&=\mathbb{E}[{\rm{exp}}(\bar{Y}\log(1+\chi^{2}(P,Q)))]-1\\
&\leq{\rm{exp}}\left(\chi^{2}(P,Q)\right)-1\leq 2\cdot\chi^{2}(P,Q).\end{split}

Here (a) is due to (
91
)(
93
) and the last inequality is because
e^{x}\leq 1+2x
for
x\in[0,1]
. Finally, since
{\rm{TV}}(P,Q)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\chi^{2}(P,Q)}{2}}
by
Tsybakov 2009
Lemma 2.7, we have

{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}),Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\chi^{2}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}),Q^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)}{2}}\leq\sqrt{\chi^{2}(P,Q)}.

This has finished the proof.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
16

First notice
\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}
is an orthogonal matrix. If
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{0}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
then by the orthogonal invariant property of Gaussian, it is easy to check
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{0}^{\sigma^{\otimes d}}\times_{i}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. So the property 1 of the Lemma is established.

Now when
{\mathbfcal{W}}={\mathbfcal{W}}_{0}\sim\lambda\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}
where
{\mathbfcal{Z}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
, we first consider its update after one step.

\begin{split}{\mathbfcal{W}}^{\prime}=&{\mathbfcal{W}}^{\sigma^{\otimes d}}\times_{1}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\times\cdots\times_{d}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
=&\lambda\times_{1}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\sigma}\times\cdots\times_{d}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}_{d}^{\sigma}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}^{\sigma^{\otimes d}}\times_{1}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\times\cdots\times_{d}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
=&\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}^{d}}\times_{1}({\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}})\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\sigma}\times\cdots\times_{d}({\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}})\mathbf{u}_{d}^{\sigma}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}^{\sigma^{\otimes d}}\times_{1}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\times\cdots\times_{d}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}.\end{split}

(94)

By the result of the first part, we know
{\mathbfcal{Z}}^{\sigma^{\otimes d}}\times_{1}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\times\cdots\times_{d}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}.
Now we consider the first term at the right hand side of (
94
). Denote
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(0)}=\mathbf{u}_{i}
and
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}=({\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}})(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})^{\sigma},\,(1\leq i\leq d)
. Since
\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}
is orthogonal,

\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}=2\|(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})^{\sigma}\|_{2}^{2}=2\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell-1)}\|_{2}^{2}.

After
\ell
iterations, we have
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}=2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
for
1\leq i\leq d
. Also
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
since each entry of
({\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}})
belongs to
\{-1,0,1\}
.
By the definition of
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}
, after
\ell
steps, we have

\varphi\left(\lambda\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\sim\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}^{d\ell}}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}^{(\ell)}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}^{(\ell)}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right).

The first part of statement (2) follows.

At the same time if
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{u}_{j}
for
i,j\in[d],i\neq j
, then
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{u}_{j}^{(\ell)}
holds almost surely by the definition of
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}
.

To finish the proof of the statement (2), we only need to show the desired bound for
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{0},(1\leq i\leq d)
. Since
\frac{{\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}}{\sqrt{2}}
only has two non-zero values for each row and each column,
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}\leq 2\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell-1)}\|_{0}
. Iterate this step, we have
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}\leq 2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{u}_{i}\|_{0}
. The proof for the lower bound of
\|\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}
follows the same as the one of Lemma 26 in
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2018
and we omit it here for simplicity. This has finished the proof.

D
Proofs of Computational Lower Bounds of CHC
D
, CHC
R

D.1
Proof of Theorem
15

The high level idea to show the computational lower bound of
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}
is given in Section
5.4
. The randomized polynomial-time reduction procedure we use shares the same idea as
Ma and Wu 2015
, but are modified to handle high-order case. Also we note applying tensor version Gaussian distributional lifting and multivariate rejection kernel techniques in
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2018
;
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2019
could probably also yield the same tight computational lower bound.

First, we introduce a few necessary notation. Let
N=dn\ell
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
to be chosen depending on
n,k,\lambda
of CHC model and
{\mathbfcal{A}}\in(\{0,1\}^{N})^{\otimes d}
be the adjacency tensor of hypergraph
G
.

Algorithm 10

Randomized Polynomial-time Reduction for CHC Detection

1:

Input:

{\mathbfcal{A}}\in(\{0,1\}^{N})^{\otimes d}
.

2:

Let
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{0}={\mathbfcal{A}}_{[1:n\ell,n\ell+1:2n\ell,\ldots,(d-1)n\ell+1:dn\ell]}
. Let
{\rm{RK}}_{G}={\rm{RK}}(1\to N(\xi,1),\frac{1}{2}\to N(0,1),T)
with
T=\lceil 2(d+1)\log_{2}(n\ell)\rceil
and
\xi=\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log(n\ell)+2\log 2}}
. Compute the tensor
{\mathbfcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{(n\ell)}^{\otimes d}}
with
{\mathbfcal{B}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}={\rm{RK}}_{G}({\mathbfcal{A}}_{0[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]})
.

3:

Construct
{\mathbfcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{n}^{\otimes d}}
as follows:

{\mathbfcal{Y}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}=\frac{1}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{j_{1}\in[\ell]}\cdots\sum_{j_{d}\in[\ell]}{\mathbfcal{B}}_{[(j_{1}-1)n+i_{1},\ldots,(j_{d}-1)n+i_{d}]},\quad 1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\leq n.

(95)

4:

Output:

{\mathbfcal{Y}}
.

Algorithm
10
summaries the randomized polynomial-time reduction procedure:

\begin{split}\varphi:(\{0,1\}^{N})^{\otimes d}\to\mathbb{R}^{{n}^{\otimes d}}:{\mathbfcal{A}}\to{\mathbfcal{Y}}.\end{split}

(96)

Lemma
17
shows that the randomized polynomial-time algorithm we construct in Algorithm
10
maps
{\rm{HPC}}_{D}
to
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}
asymptotically.

Lemma 17

Given hypergraph
G
and its adjacency tensor
{\mathbfcal{A}}
. When
N=dn\ell
for some
\ell>0
integer, if
G\sim H_{0}^{G}
,

\displaystyle{\rm{TV}}(N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})},\mathcal{L}(\varphi({\mathbfcal{A}})))\leq\frac{1}{n}.

(97)

If
G\sim H_{1}^{G}
, and in addition assume
\xi=\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log(n\ell)+2\log 2}}
,
\kappa\geq 4d,\kappa=4dk,n\geq 2\kappa
, then there exists a prior
\pi
on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{(\frac{N}{dn})^{d/2}}}
such that

{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}(\varphi({\mathbfcal{A}})),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})\leq\frac{1}{n}+2d\,{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{2d^{2}}\kappa)+dk\,{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{4d}\kappa\log\frac{n}{k}),

(98)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\cdot)=\int_{\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{(\frac{N}{dn})^{d/2}}}}\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{X}}}(\cdot)d\pi({\mathbfcal{X}})
, and
c>0
is some fixed constant.

Lemma
17
specifically implies that if
N=dn\ell
,
\kappa\geq 4d,\kappa=4dk,n\geq 2\kappa
and
\mu=\frac{\xi}{(\frac{N}{dn})^{d/2}}
with
\xi=\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log(n\ell)+2\log 2}}
, the reduction map
\varphi(G)
we construct from Algorithm
10
satisfies

TV(\varphi({\rm{HPC}}_{D}(N,1/2,\kappa)),{\rm{CHC}}_{D}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mu))\to 0

under both
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
.

Now we prove the computational lower bound of CHC detection by contradiction. If when
\beta>(\alpha-\frac{1}{2})d\lor 0=:\beta^{c}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}
, there exists a sequence of algorithm
\{\phi\}_{n}
such that
\liminf\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{n})<\frac{1}{2}
. Then under this regime, we can find
\lambda
such that

\lambda\leq\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log(n\ell)+2\log 2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda\leq\frac{Cn^{\frac{d}{2}}}{k^{d+\delta}},

(99)

for some
\delta>0
. Here we let the sparsity and signal strength of the
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}
satisfy
k=\tilde{\Theta}(n^{\alpha})
,
\lambda=\tilde{\Theta}(n^{-\beta})
.

Under the first condition in (
99
), there exists
\ell\geq 1
such that
\lambda\leq\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}=\frac{\xi}{(N/(dn))^{\frac{d}{2}}}
by the definition of
\xi
. Thus
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{(\frac{N}{dn})^{d/2}}}
is supported on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda}
and let
\ell
to be the largest integer satisfies
\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}\geq\lambda
.

By combining Lemma
17
and Lemma
11
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{HPC}}_{D}}(\phi_{n}\circ\varphi)\leq\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{n})+\frac{2}{n}+2d{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{2d^{2}}\kappa)+dk\,{\rm{exp}}(-k\log\frac{n}{k}),

and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{HPC}}_{D}}(\phi_{n}\circ\varphi)<\frac{1}{2}
, i.e.
\phi_{n}\circ\varphi
has asymptotic risk less than
\frac{1}{2}
in HPC detection.

On the other hand, note
d
is fixed and
\ell
is a largest integer satisfying
\lambda\leq\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}
, so combining with the second condition in (
99
), we have

\frac{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\xi}\geq\frac{k^{d+\delta}}{Cn^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Longrightarrow k^{d+\delta}\leq CN^{\frac{d}{2}}\sqrt{2(d+1)\log N}\Longrightarrow\left(\frac{\kappa}{4d}\right)^{d+\delta}\leq CN^{\frac{d}{2}}\sqrt{2(d+1)\log N},

which implies
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\kappa}{\log N}\leq\frac{1}{2+\frac{\delta}{2}}<\frac{1}{2}
. The above two facts together contradicts with Conjecture
1
. So by contradiction argument, we have finished the proof.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
17

First, by Lemma
15
, when
\xi=\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log(n\ell)+2\log 2}}
, we have

{\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(1)),N(\xi,1)\right)=O({(n\ell)}^{-(d+1)})\quad\text{and }{\rm{TV}}({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2})),N(0,1))=O({(n\ell)}^{-(d+1)}).

(100)

Under
H_{0}^{G}
, let
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{(n\ell)}^{\otimes d}}
be a random tensor with i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries independent of
{\mathbfcal{A}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
be the quantity by applying (
95
) to
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
. It is straightforward to verify
\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
has i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{Y}}})=N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
. So we have

{\rm{TV}}(N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})},\mathcal{L}(\varphi({\mathbfcal{A}})))\leq{\rm{TV}}(\widetilde{{\mathbfcal{B}}},{\mathbfcal{B}})\leq(n\ell)^{d}(n\ell)^{-(d+1)}=\frac{1}{n},

(101)

where the first inequality is due to Lemma
12
and the second inequality is due to the tensorization and (
100
).

Under
H_{1}^{G}
, we denote the index set of the planted clique to be
V
. Let
V_{1}=V\bigcap[n\ell],V_{2}=\left(V\bigcap[n\ell+1:2n\ell]\right)-n\ell,\ldots,V_{d}=\left(V\bigcap[(d-1)n\ell+1,dn\ell]\right)-(d-1)n\ell
, here the notation “a set minus a value" means shift the indices of the set by that value. By assumption, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{d}|V_{i}|=\kappa
. Recall
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{0}={\mathbfcal{A}}_{[1:n\ell,n\ell+1:2n\ell,\ldots,(d-1)n\ell+1:dn\ell]}
, so
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{0_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}}=1
if
[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]\in V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{d}
and
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{0_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}}\sim{\rm{Bern}}(1/2)
otherwise.

Denote the map
h(x):=1+(x-1)\text{ mod }n
. Let

U_{i}=h(V_{i}),\,i=1,2,\ldots,d,

(102)

where
U_{i}
could be viewed as the latent signal support on
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
.

We define sets

\begin{split}&N_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}:=[h^{-1}(i_{1}),\ldots,h^{-1}(i_{d})]\setminus(V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{d})\\
&T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}:=[h^{-1}(i_{1}),\ldots,h^{-1}(i_{d})]\bigcap(V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{d})\end{split}

(103)

for
1\leq i_{j}\leq n\,(1\leq j\leq d)
. By construction, we have
{\mathbfcal{Y}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}
is the normalized summation of values of
{\mathbfcal{B}}
on sets
N_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}
and
T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}
.

We divide the rest of the proof into two steps:

Step 1.
In this step we show the event

E=\{|U_{1}|\geq k,|U_{2}|\geq k,\ldots,|U_{d}|\geq k\},

happens with high probability.

To show this, we first show
|V_{1}|,\ldots,|V_{d}|
are concentrated around
\frac{\kappa}{2d}
. By symmetry, we only need to consider
|V_{1}|
. Notice
|V_{1}|
follows the hypergeometric distribution
H\mathcal{G}(N,\kappa,\frac{N}{d})
. By concentration result of hypergeometric distribution in
Hush and Scovel 2005
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\left||V_{1}|-\frac{\kappa}{d}\right|\geq\frac{\kappa}{2d})\leq{\rm{exp}}(-2\alpha_{h}(\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4d^{2}}-1))\leq{\rm{exp}}\left(-\frac{c}{2d^{2}}\kappa\right),

(104)

for some constant
c>0
and
\alpha_{h}:=\max(\frac{1}{N/d+1}+\frac{1}{\frac{d-1}{d}N+1},\frac{1}{\kappa+1}+\frac{1}{N-\kappa+1})
. The last inequality is due to the fact that when
N\geq dn
and
n\geq 2\kappa
,
\alpha_{h}
is of order
\frac{1}{\kappa+1}
.

Since
\frac{\kappa}{d}=4k
,
\frac{\kappa}{2d}\asymp\frac{3\kappa}{2d}\asymp k
, then for
\kappa_{1}\in[\frac{\kappa}{2d},\frac{3\kappa}{2d}]
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(|U_{1}|\leq k||V_{1}|=\kappa_{1})&\leq\sum_{j=\lceil\kappa_{1}/\ell\rceil}^{k}\frac{{n\choose j}{j\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}{{n\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}\\
&\leq k\frac{{n\choose k}{k\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}{{n\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}\leq k(\frac{en}{k})^{k}(\frac{ek\ell}{\kappa_{1}})^{\kappa_{1}}(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{n\ell-\kappa_{1}})^{\kappa_{1}}\\
&\leq k{\rm{exp}}\left(k\log\frac{en}{k}-\kappa_{1}\log(\frac{n\ell-\kappa_{1}}{ek\ell})\right)\\
&\leq k{\rm{exp}}(k\log\frac{en}{k}-\frac{\kappa}{2d}\log(\frac{n\ell}{k\ell}))\\
&\leq k{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{4d}\kappa\log\frac{n}{k}),\end{split}

(105)

for some
c>0
and here the first inequality is due to the fact that
j\longmapsto\frac{{n\choose j}{j\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}{{n\ell\choose\kappa_{1}}}
is an increasing function of
j
when
n\geq 2\kappa
and the last inequality is because
k=\frac{\kappa}{4d}
.
So

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(|U_{1}|\leq k)&\leq\sum_{\kappa_{1}=0}^{\kappa}\mathbb{P}(|U_{1}|\leq k||V_{1}|=\kappa_{1})\mathbb{P}(|V_{1}|=\kappa_{1})\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(|V_{1}|\leq\frac{\kappa}{2d})+\mathbb{P}(|V_{1}|\geq\frac{3}{2d}\kappa)+\max_{\kappa_{1}\in[\frac{\kappa}{2d},\frac{3\kappa}{2d}]}\mathbb{P}(|U_{1}|\leq k||V_{1}|=\kappa)\\
&\leq 2{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{2d^{2}}\kappa)+k{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{4d}\kappa\log\frac{n}{k}).\end{split}

(106)

Thus

\mathbb{P}(E^{c})=\mathbb{P}\left(\left(\bigcap_{i}\{U_{i}\geq k\}\right)^{c}\right)\leq 2d\,{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{2d^{2}}\kappa)+dk\,{\rm{exp}}(-\frac{c}{4d}\kappa\log\frac{n}{k}),

(107)

for some
c>0
.

Step 2.
Now condition on
V
, generate
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{(n\ell)}^{\otimes d}}
random tensor such that
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}_{[V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}]}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}N(\xi,1)
and the rest of the entries of
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
. Denote
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
as the quantity of applying
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
to (
95
) of Algorithm
10
.
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}
and
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
are the ideal values we want
{\mathbfcal{B}}
and
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
to be under
H_{1}^{G}
.

By the construction of
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
, for any
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}]=\frac{\xi|T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}|}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}
. Since for any
[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]\in V_{1}\times V_{2}\times\cdots\times V_{d}
,
|T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}|\geq 1
, in this case
\mathbb{E}[\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}]\geq\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}}
.

Also by the construction of
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
, we have the entries of
\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}
are independent. Since
1_{E}
is deterministic given
V
, for any
V
such that
1_{E}=1
, there exists some
{\mathbfcal{X}}={\mathbfcal{X}}(V)\in\mathscr{X}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}})
such that
\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}|V)=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{X}}}
.

Define the probability distribution
\pi=\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{X}}(V)|E)
which is supported on the set
\mathscr{X}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}})
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}|E)=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
is a mixture of distributions of
\{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{X}}}:{\mathbfcal{X}}\in\mathscr{X}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\frac{\xi}{\ell^{\frac{d}{2}}})\}
.

Now we are ready to show that
{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{Y}}),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})
is small.

\begin{split}{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{Y}}),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})&\overset{(a)}{\leq}{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{Y}}),\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}))+{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{V}[{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{Y}}|V),\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}|V))]+{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}),\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{Y}}}|E))\\
&\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}=1}^{n\ell}{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{B}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}|V),\mathcal{L}(\widecheck{{\mathbfcal{B}}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}|V))+\mathbb{P}(E^{c})\\
&\overset{(c)}{\leq}(n\ell)^{d}(n\ell)^{-(d+1)}+\mathbb{P}(E^{c})\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n}+\mathbb{P}(E^{c}),\end{split}

(108)

where (a) is due to triangle inequality, (b) is due to Lemma
12
and Lemma 7 of
Brennan, Bresler and
Huleihel 2018
and (c) is due to (
100
). This has finished the proof of this lemma.

D.3
Proof of Theorem
16

In this section, we prove the computational lower bound for
{\rm{CHC}}_{R}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)
and it is enough to prove the lower bound for the case:

{\mathbfcal{X}}\in\left\{\lambda\mathbf{1}_{I_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{I_{d}}:I_{i}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n_{i}\},|I_{i}|=k_{i}\right\}.

We first introduce a randomized polynomial-time Algorithm
11
to do reduction from
{\rm{HPDS}}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2})
to
{\rm{CHC}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\lambda)
.

Algorithm 11

Randomized Polynomial-time Reduction for CHC Recovery

1:

Input:
Hypergraph
G
and the density bias
\rho
.

2:

Let
{\rm{RK}}_{G}={\rm{RK}}(\frac{1}{2}+\rho\to N(\xi,1),\frac{1}{2}\to N(0,1),T)
where
\xi=\frac{\log(1+2\rho)}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log 2}}
and
T=\lceil 2(d+1)\log_{1+2\rho}n\rceil
and compute the symmetric tensor
{\mathbfcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
with
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}={\rm{RK}}_{G}(\mathbf{1}((i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in E(G)))
. Let the diagonal entries of
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{[i,\ldots,i]}
to be i.i.d.
N(0,1)
.

3:

Generate
(d!-1)
i.i.d. symmetric random tensor
{\mathbfcal{B}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\mathbfcal{B}}^{(d!-1)}
in the following way: their diagonal values are
0
and non-diagonal values are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
. Given any non-diagonal index
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
(
i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{d}
), suppose it has
D\,(D\leq d!)
unique permutations and denote them as
\mathbf{i}_{(0)}:=\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{i}_{(D-1)}
, then we transform
{\mathbfcal{W}}
in the following way

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathbfcal{W}}_{\mathbf{i}_{(0)}}\\
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{\mathbf{i}_{(1)}}\\
\vdots\\
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{\mathbf{i}_{(D-1)}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\sqrt{d!}},\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\sqrt{d!}}\right)_{\perp}\right)_{[1:D,:]}\times\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathbfcal{W}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}\\
{\mathbfcal{B}}^{(1)}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}\\
\vdots\\
{\mathbfcal{B}}^{(d!-1)}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}\end{array}\right).

Here
\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\sqrt{d!}}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{d!}
vector with all entries to be
\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}
and
\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\sqrt{d!}}\right)_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{d!\times(d!-1)}
is any orthogonal complement of
\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\sqrt{d!}}
.

4:

Output:

{\mathbfcal{W}}^{{\rm{id}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}}
, where
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}
are independent permutations of
[n]
chosen uniformly at random.

The Lemma
18
shows randomized polynomial-time mapping in Algorithm
11
maps HPDS to CHC asymptotically.

Lemma 18

Suppose that
n,\xi
and
\rho\geq\frac{1}{n^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}
are such that

\xi=\frac{\log(1+2\rho)}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log 2}},

then the randomized polynomial-time computable map
\varphi:\mathcal{G}_{d}(n)\to\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
represented by Algorithm
11
holds the following: for any subset
S\subseteq[n]
with
|S|=\kappa
,

\begin{split}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\varphi\left(\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2},S)\right),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)d\pi(T_{1})\cdots d\pi(T_{d-1})\right)\\
&=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}\right),\end{split}

where
{\mathbfcal{Z}}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
and
\pi
is the uniform distribution on subsets of
[n]
of size
\kappa
and
\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2},S)
represents the distribution of HPDS
\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2})
with planted dense subgraph supported on set
S
.

Lemma
18
specifically implies that if
k=\kappa
,
\lambda=\frac{\log(1+2\rho)}{2\sqrt{d!}\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log 2}}
, the reduction map
\varphi(G)
in Algorithm
11
satisfies

{\rm{TV}}(\varphi({\rm{HPDS}}_{R}(n,\kappa,1/2+\rho,1/2)),\mathcal{L}({\rm{CHC}}_{R}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\lambda)))\to 0.

Next, we prove Theorem
16
by contradiction argument and we divide the proof into dense and sparse regimes.

•

\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Let
k=\kappa\lceil n^{\alpha}\rceil
and
\rho=n^{-\beta}
. Then it is easy to check in CHC model, the signal strength and sparsity levels are
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log(\lambda^{-1})}{\log n}=\beta
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log k}{\log n}=\alpha
.

Suppose when
(d-1)\alpha-\frac{d-1}{2}<\beta
, there is a sequence of polynomial-time algorithm
\{\phi\}_{n}
such that w.p. more than
\frac{1}{2}
, it can identify the true planted latent cluster in
{\mathbfcal{Y}}\sim{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\lambda)
. Denote the support in
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
as
S
. Let
\phi^{1}
be the restriction of
\phi
that only output the estimated support of
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
at mode 1.

Denote
\mathcal{L}_{S}
as the distribution of

\int\mathcal{L}\left(\lambda\cdot\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)d\pi(T_{1})\cdots d\pi(T_{d-1}),

where
\pi
is the uniform distribution on the subset of
[n]
of size
\kappa
. By Lemma
18
and noticing
\lambda=\xi/\sqrt{d!}
, we have

\left|\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\varphi(G))}\left(\phi_{n}^{1}({\mathbfcal{W}})=S\right)-\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}\sim\mathcal{L}_{S}}(\phi_{n}^{1}({\mathbfcal{W}})=S)\right|\leq{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}(\varphi(G)),\mathcal{L}_{S})=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}),

where the inequality is due to the definition of total variation distance.

Note by assumption

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}\sim\mathcal{L}_{S}}(\phi_{n}^{1}({\mathbfcal{W}})=S)=\mathbb{E}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{d-1}\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}\sim\mathcal{L}_{S,T_{1},\ldots,T_{d-1}}}(\phi_{n}^{1}({\mathbfcal{W}})=S)>\frac{1}{2},

so

\mathbb{P}\left(\phi^{1}\circ\varphi(G)=S\right)\geq\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbfcal{W}}\sim\mathcal{L}_{S}}(\phi_{n}^{1}({\mathbfcal{W}})=S)-O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}),

and
\liminf\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{HPDS}}_{R}}(\phi^{1}\circ\varphi)<\frac{1}{2}
, i.e.
\phi^{1}\circ\varphi
can recovery the support of HPDS with asymptotic risk less than
\frac{1}{2}
.

On the other hand, the condition (
24
) at here becomes

\lim_{n\to\infty}\log_{n}\frac{\kappa^{d-1}\rho}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-\rho^{2}}}=(d-1)\alpha-\beta<\frac{d-1}{2},

where the inequality is because
(d-1)\alpha-\frac{d-1}{2}<\beta
. Combining
1/2+\rho<1-\Omega(1)
, these two facts together contradict the HPDS recovery conjecture
2
. This has finished the proof for the
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}
region.

•

0<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
. The main idea to prove the computational lower bound for
{\rm{CHC}}_{R}
in this regime is to use the established computational lower bound for
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}
. Suppose
\beta>0
(for example, we can let
\beta=\epsilon
for some small enough
\epsilon>0
), there is a sequence of polynomial-time algorithm
\{\phi_{R}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{R}}(\phi_{R})<\frac{1}{2}
. In this regime, we have
\lambda\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}}\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}
, then by the following Lemma
19
, there exists a sequence of polynomial-time test
\{\phi_{D}\}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<\frac{1}{2}
. This contradicts with the computational lower bound of
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}
under the HPC conjecture
1
. So this has established the computational lower bound for
{\rm{CHC}}_{R}
in regime
0<\alpha<1/2
.

In summary, combining the results in dense and sparse regime, we have established the computational lower bound for
{\rm{CHC}}_{R}
in the regime
\beta>\beta^{c}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{R}}:=(d-1)(\alpha-1/2)\vee 0
.

Lemma 19

Consider
{\rm{CHC}}_{D}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)
and
{\rm{CHC}}_{R}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n},\lambda)
under the asymptotic regime (
A1
). If
\lambda\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}}\geq Ck^{\frac{d}{4}}
for some
C>0
and there exists a sequence of polynomial-time recovery algorithm
\{\phi_{R}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{R}}(\phi_{R})<\eta
for some
\eta\in(0,1]
, then there exists a sequence of polynomial-time algorithm
\{\phi_{D}\}_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\rm{CHC}}_{D}}(\phi_{D})<\eta
.

D.4
Proof of Lemma
18

Let
\varphi
be the map of Algorithm
11
and
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{2}
,
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{3}
,
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{4}
be the value of
{\mathbfcal{W}}
after step 2, step 3 and step 4 of
\varphi
.

First by Lemma
15
, we know that with value
\xi=\frac{\log(1+2\rho)}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log 2}}
and
\rho\geq\frac{1}{n^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}
,
{\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2}+\rho)),N(\xi,1)\right)=O(n^{-(d+1)})
and
{\rm{TV}}({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2}),N(0,1)))=O(n^{-(d+1)}).

Denote
\mathcal{M}_{n}:=\mathcal{M}_{n}(S,P,Q)
as the distribution of a
\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
symmetric tensor
{\mathbfcal{A}}
, where its diagonal entries are independent
N(0,1)
random variables, and for non-diagonal entries
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}
, if set
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\subseteq S
,
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}
is draw independently from distribution
P
and if
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\nsubseteq S
,
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]}
is draw independently from
Q
. Here
S
is a subset of
[n]
of size
\kappa
.

Let
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{2}\sim\mathcal{M}_{n}(S,N(\xi,1),N(0,1))
and
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{3}
,
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
be the value of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{2}
after applying step 3 and step 3 and 4 of
\varphi
to
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{2}
. Here
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{2},{\mathbfcal{M}}_{3},{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
could be viewed as the ideal values we want
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{2},{\mathbfcal{W}}_{3},{\mathbfcal{W}}_{4}
to be.

After step 2 of
\varphi
,

\begin{split}{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{2}),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{2})\right)\leq&\kappa^{d}{\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2}+\rho)),N(\xi,1)\right)\\
&+\left(n^{d}-\kappa^{d}\right){\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2}),N(0,1))\right)\\
=&O(\frac{1}{n}),\end{split}

where
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2},S)
.

Also by data processing inequality, we have

\begin{split}{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{3}),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{3})\right)&\leq{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{2}),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{2})\right)=O(n^{-1}).\end{split}

(109)

Also notice that after applying step 3 of
\varphi
, the diagonal values of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{3}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
and the non-diagonal values of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{3}
have the same distribution as the non-diagonal entries of
\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{S}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}
.

Now consider the distribution of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
condition on permutations
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}
. It’s entries have the ditribution as entries of

\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})},

other than entries at indices
(i,\sigma_{1}(i),\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(i))
for
i\in S
. Here
T_{1}=\sigma_{1}(S),\ldots,T_{d-1}=\sigma_{d-1}(S)
. Specifically, conditioning on
\sigma_{1}(S)=T_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(S)=T_{d-1}
, we have

\begin{split}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{4})|\sigma_{1}(S)=T_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(S)=T_{d-1}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)\right)\\
&={\rm{TV}}\left(\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{4})[S\times T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{d-1}]|\sigma_{1}(S)=T_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1}(S)=T_{d-1}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(N(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}},1)^{\otimes k^{\otimes d}}\right)\right)\\
&\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sqrt{\chi^{2}\left(N(0,1),N(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}})\right)}\\
&=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}).\end{split}

(110)

Here
(a)
is due to Lemma
14
.

Finally, we have

\begin{split}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\varphi\left(\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2},S)\right),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)d\pi(T_{1})\cdots d\pi(T_{d-1})\right)\\
\overset{(a)}{\leq}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\varphi\left(\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\kappa,\frac{1}{2}+\rho,\frac{1}{2},S)\right),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{4})\right)\\
&+{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)d\pi(T_{1})\cdots d\pi(T_{d-1})\right)\\
\overset{(b)}{\leq}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{3}),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{3})\right)\\
&+{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{S}\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d-1}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}\right)d\pi(T_{1})\cdots d\pi(T_{d-1})\right)\\
\overset{(c)}{\leq}&O(\frac{1}{n})+O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}})=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}),\end{split}

where
(a)
is due to triangle inequality, (b) is due to data processing inequality and (c) is due to (
109
)(
110
). So we finish the proof of this Lemma.

D.5
Proof of Lemma
19

The proof idea is similar to the proof of statement (2) of Lemma
10
. Given
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
generated from

\mathcal{L}\left(\lambda\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{I_{d}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}\right),

by the property of Gaussian, it is easy to check that
{\mathbfcal{A}}:=\frac{{\mathbfcal{Y}}+{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
and
{\mathbfcal{B}}:=\frac{{\mathbfcal{Y}}-{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
are two independent copies with distribution

\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{I_{d}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}\right),

if
{\mathbfcal{Z}}_{1}\sim N(0,1)^{\otimes n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}
and independent of
{\mathbfcal{Y}}
.

The rest of the proof is the same as the proof of statement (2) of Lemma
10
by replacing
\mu
with
\lambda\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}}
.

E
Proofs of Computational Lower Bounds of
{\rm{ROHC}}_{D}
and
{\rm{ROHC}}_{R}

E.1
Proof of Lemma
2

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{G}_{d}(n)\to\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes d}}
be the map in Algorithm
8
and let
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{4}
,
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{5}
be the values of
{\mathbfcal{W}}
after step 4 and step 5 of Algorithm
8
. Also by Lemma
15
, we know that with value
\xi=\frac{\log 2}{2\sqrt{2(d+1)\log n+2\log 2}}
, we have

{\rm{TV}}\left({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(1)),N(\xi,1)\right)=O(n^{-(d+1)}),\,\text{and }{\rm{TV}}({\rm{RK}}_{G}({\rm{Bern}}(\frac{1}{2}),N(0,1)))=O(n^{-(d+1)}).

(111)

If
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\frac{1}{2})
, then

{\rm{TV}}\left(\varphi(\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\frac{1}{2})),N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\leq{\rm{TV}}\left(\varphi_{1}(\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\frac{1}{2})),N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)\leq O(\frac{1}{n}),

where
\varphi_{1}
denotes the step 1 of
\varphi
, the first inequality is due to data processing inequality and the second one is due to tensorization and (
111
).

Now we consider the case
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{d}(n,\frac{1}{2},\kappa)
. First following the same proof of Lemma
18
, we have

\begin{split}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{4}),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)d\pi^{\prime}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\right)\\
=&O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}\right),\end{split}

(112)

where
\pi^{\prime}
is the uniform distributions over pairs
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
of
\kappa
-subsets
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\subseteq[n]
.

Let
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
be a tensor distributed as

\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{T_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{1}_{T_{d}}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})},

where
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}
are
\kappa
-subsets of
[n]
chosen uniformly at random. Also let
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}
be the value of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
after applying step 5 of
\varphi
to
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
. Again
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{4}
and
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}
are the ideal values we want
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{4}
and
{\mathbfcal{W}}_{5}
to be. By statement 2 of Lemma
16
, the distribution of
{\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}
condition on sets
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}
is given by

\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}|T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\sim\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}(\sqrt{2})^{d\ell}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{v}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)d\bar{\pi}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{d}),

where
\bar{\pi}:=\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}}
is a prior defined in Lemma
16
.

As shown in Lemma
16
,
\mathbf{v}_{i}
s
(1\leq i\leq d)
are supported on
\bar{\pi}
and satisfy
\|\mathbf{v}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=2^{\ell}\|\mathbf{1}_{T_{i}}\|_{2}^{2}=2^{\ell}\kappa
,
\|\mathbf{v}_{i}\|_{0}\leq 2^{\ell}\kappa
. Moreover, with probability at least
1-4d/\kappa
on
\bar{\pi}
, we have
\|\mathbf{v}_{i}\|_{0}\geq 2^{\ell}\kappa(1-\max(\frac{2C\ell\log(2^{\ell}\kappa)}{\kappa},\frac{2^{\ell}\kappa}{n}))
. We denote this conditional distribution of
\bar{\pi}
on this event as
\bar{\pi}^{\prime}
. Since
2^{\ell}\kappa\leq n/C
for
C>1
, if
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell}\kappa}}\mathbf{v}_{i}
, then
\bar{\pi}^{\prime}
induces a prior on pair
(\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d})
in
\mathcal{V}_{n,2^{\ell}\kappa}
. This is because
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as shown in Lemma
16
, and the nonzero entries of
\mathbf{u}_{i}
have magnitudes at least
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell}\kappa}}
.

Let
\pi^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}}\left(\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}}\right)
be a prior formed by marginalizing
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}
, and
\pi^{\prime}
is also supported on
\mathcal{V}_{n,2^{\ell}\kappa}
. So

\begin{split}{\rm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\kappa^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes(n^{\otimes d})}\right)d\pi^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}))\leq 4d/\kappa.\end{split}

(113)

Finally by triangle inequality and Lemma
12
, we have

\begin{split}&{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\varphi(G)),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi\kappa^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}+N(0,1)^{\otimes p^{(\otimes d)}}\right)d\pi^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d})\right)\\
\leq&{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{W}}_{5}),\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{5})\right)\\
&+{\rm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}({\mathbfcal{M}}_{5}),\int\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{d!}}\kappa^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathbf{u}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}_{d}\right)d\pi^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d})\right)\\
\overset{(a)}{\leq}&O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log n}}),\end{split}

where (a) is due to (
112
), (
113
). This has finished the proof.

F
Proofs for the Evidence of HPC Conjecture
1
and HPDS Conjecture
2

F.1
Proof of Proposition
1

Without loss of generality, we can assume
N
is a multiplier of
d
, otherwise we can replace
N=d\lfloor\frac{N}{d}\rfloor
. Recall the planted dense subgraph index set is
K
, and let
K_{i}=K\bigcap[(i-1)\frac{N}{d}+1:\frac{iN}{d}]
. By symmetry, we only need to consider recovering
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
.

First by the same argument as (
104
), we can show
|K_{1}|\asymp|K_{2}|\asymp\frac{\kappa}{d}
. Denote
\{X_{i}^{(1)}\}
as i.i.d.
{\rm{Bern}}(q_{2})
random variables and
\{X_{i}^{(2)}\}
as i.i.d.
{\rm{Bern}}(q_{1})
random variables. Following the same notation in Algorithm
5
, for
(k_{1},k_{2})\notin K_{1}\times K_{2}
,

{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,2)}_{[k_{1},k_{2}]}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{(\frac{N}{d})^{d-2}}\left(X_{i}^{(1)}-q_{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{N}{d}\right)^{d-2}\left(q_{2}(1-q_{2})\right)}},

and for
(k_{1},k_{2})\in K_{1}\times K_{2}
,

{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,2)}_{[k_{1},k_{2}]}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{(\frac{N}{d})^{d-2}-C(\frac{\kappa}{d})^{d-2}}\left(X_{i}^{(1)}-q_{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{N}{d}\right)^{d-2}\left(q_{2}(1-q_{2})\right)}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{C(\frac{\kappa}{d})^{d-2}}\left(X_{i}^{(2)}-q_{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{N}{d}\right)^{d-2}\left(q_{2}(1-q_{2})\right)}},

for some constant
C>0
.

By Chernoff bound, we have if
(k_{1},k_{2})\notin K_{1}\times K_{2}

\mathbb{P}\left(|{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,2)}_{[k_{1},k_{2}]}|>t\right)\leq{\rm{exp}}(-t^{2}),

and if
(k_{1},k_{2})\in K_{1}\times K_{2}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,2)}_{[k_{1},k_{2}]}-C\frac{(\frac{\kappa}{d})^{d-2}(q_{1}-q_{2})}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{N}{d}\right)^{d-2}\left(q_{2}(1-q_{2})\right)}}\right|>t\right)\leq{\rm{exp}}(-t^{2})

So we can write
{\mathbf{Y}}=\lambda\cdot 1_{K_{1}}1_{K_{2}}^{\top}+{\mathbf{Z}}
where
\lambda=C\frac{(\frac{\kappa}{d})^{d-2}(q_{1}-q_{2})}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{N}{d}\right)^{d-2}\left(q_{2}(1-q_{2})\right)}}
and entries
{\mathbf{Z}}_{ij}
are independent subgaussian random variable with variance
1
.

To recover
K_{1},K_{2}
, it is the same as biclustering recovery problem studied in literature with parameters
(\frac{N}{d},|K_{1}|,|K_{2}|,\lambda)
. By Lemma 1 of
Cai, Liang and
Rakhlin 2017
, when

\lambda\geq C^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{\frac{N}{d}}}{|K_{1}|\land|K_{2}|},\text{ i.e., }\limsup_{N\to\infty}\log_{N}\left(\frac{\kappa^{d-1}(q_{1}-q_{2})}{\sqrt{q_{2}(1-q_{2})}}\right)\geq\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2},

then with probability at least
1-(\frac{N}{d})^{-c}-{\rm{exp}}(-C\frac{N}{d})
, the output of the Algorithm can exactly recover
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
. Similar analysis holds for other modes.

However when
\limsup_{N\to\infty}\log_{N}\left(\frac{\kappa^{d-1}(q_{1}-q_{2})}{\sqrt{q_{2}(1-q_{2})}}\right)<\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2}
, then under a variant PC recovery conjecture
Cai, Liang and
Rakhlin 2017
, the biclustering recovery procedure of Algorithm
5
fails with non-trivial probability, so as Algorithm
6
.

F.2
Proof of Lemma
1

The proof of this lemma is by a contradiction argument. Suppose for sufficient large
N
, there is no polynomial-time recovery algorithm can output the right clique of
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N,1/2,\kappa)
with success probability at least
1-1/N
, but we can distinguish in polynomial time whether a hypergraph
G
is drawn from
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N,\frac{1}{2})
or
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N,\frac{1}{2},\kappa/3)
with probability at least
\frac{1}{4N^{d}}
. Denote
\varphi
as this distinguisher.

In Algorithm
12
, we provide an polynomial-time algorithm which can find a clique of size
\kappa
in
G
using the distinguisher
\varphi
for
G
from
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N,\frac{1}{2},\kappa)
. Denote the clique set in HPC as
K
. We first give a high level idea why the algorithm works. When
\mathbf{v}\subsetneq K
, by construction, after remove
\mathbf{v}
and
X
, at most
2\kappa/3
nodes in the clique is removed with high probability by concentration, thus the graph
G_{x}
is a random graph with a planted clique of size at least
\kappa/3
, i.e., chosen from
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N_{x},1/2,\kappa^{\prime})
for some
\kappa^{\prime}>\kappa/3
. When
\mathbf{v}\subset K
, then after remove
\mathbf{v}
and
X
, we remove the entire clique
K
, and the remaining hypergraph
G_{i}
is a random graph from
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N_{x},\frac{1}{2})
. In this case we include all vertices in
\mathbf{v}
to
Q
set.

Based on the idea above, we formalize the proof next. The proof can be divided into two steps.

Step 1.
In this step, we consider
\mathbf{v}\subsetneq K
in step 3 of Algorithm
12
. When
\mathbf{v}\subsetneq K
, then each entry of
{\mathbfcal{A}}_{[v_{1},\ldots,v_{d-1},x]}
are independent
{\rm{Bern}}(1/2)
random variables. Thus each vertex in clique will be removed with probability
1/2
. By concentration result, for each
G_{x}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\kappa^{\prime}>\frac{\kappa}{3})\geq 1-{\rm{exp}}(-C\kappa)
where
\kappa^{\prime}
is the clique size in graph
G_{x}
. So
\varphi
will output
\mathcal{G}_{d}(N_{x},1/2,\kappa/3)
with probability at least
1-{\rm{exp}}(-C\kappa)-1/(4N^{d})
.

Step 2.
In this step, we consider
\mathbf{v}\subset K
. Given
\mathbf{v}\subset K
, by the construction of Algorithm
12
, the whole clique set
K
is removed in
G_{x}
. So
G_{x}\sim\mathcal{G}_{d}(N_{i},\frac{1}{2})
, and we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\kappa^{\prime}>\frac{\kappa}{3})\leq{N_{x}\choose\kappa/3}(\frac{1}{2})^{{\kappa/3\choose d}}\leq{\rm{exp}}(-C\kappa),\end{split}

here
\kappa^{\prime}
is the clique size in graph
G_{x}
. So
\varphi
outputs
G_{d}(N_{x},\frac{1}{2})
with probability at least
1-{\rm{exp}}(-C\kappa)-\frac{1}{4N^{d}}
.

By the union bound over all possible choices of
\mathbf{v}
, there exists
C>0
that for
\kappa\geq C\log N
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(Q=K)&\geq 1-{N\choose d-1}{\rm{exp}}(-C\kappa)-{N\choose d-1}\frac{1}{2N^{d}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{N}.\end{split}

This contracts the assumption. So we have finished the proof of this lemma.

Algorithm 12

Algorithm for HPC Recovery based on HPC Detection

1:

Input:
hypergraph
G
with adjacency tensor
{\mathbfcal{A}}
, HPC detection algorithm
\varphi
.

2:

Let
H=\{\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}\subset[N],|\mathbf{v}|=d-1\}
,
Q=\emptyset
(representing the current clique).

3:

for

\mathbf{v}\in H

do

4:
